I have the honor to advise you that the proposed
visits of the above mentioned officers are agreeable
to the Department of State and that I have communicated
with the interested Federal Departments concerned with
a view to the xtension to the officers of the courtesies
and facilities usual in such cases. The Department of
State waives the passport and visa requirements to permit
their reentry into the United States to join their vessels.

CONSULATE GENERAL OF JAPAN

NEW YORK.N.Y.
May 11, 1936
Mr. Ira A. Hoyt
Passport Division
Sub-Treasury Building
New York, N. Y.
Sir:
In accordance with instructions from my
894.3311/289
government, I beg to inform you that a Japanese
training squadron consisting of two coast-defense
vessels, the "Yakumo" and the "Iwate," under the
command of Vice-Admiral Zengo Yoshida, is expected
to arrive in Mew York on the 25th of August. For
your information, I enclose herewith their itinerary
together with particulars concerning the tmo ships
and the names of prominent naval officers on board.
I am also asked to request your kind fa-
vor in extending to the training squadron and offi-
cers any possible facilities and courtesies during
their stay in your port.
If there should be any changes in the
itinerary, I should be glad to notify you.
Very truly yours,
Meonlaw Tnona
894.3311
Masutaro Inoue,
Acting Consul General.

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
PARTICULARS OF THE TWO JAPANESE
TRAINING SHIPS, THE "YAKUMO"
AND THE "IWATE", TO VISIT NEW
YORK, AUGUST 25 - SEPT. 2, 1936.
Type and Tonnage
Both the Yakumo and the Iwate are coast-defence
vessels with a speed of 16 knots and a displacement
of 9,010 and 9,180 tons respectively.
History
Launched at the turn of the century, the two ships
have distinguished records of long and honorable service.
Both vessels won glory in action in the historic battle
of the Sea of Japan in 1905 in whioh the famous Beltic
Fleet of the Russian Empire was annihilated, and both
were in active service during the World War, An out-
line history of the two ships follows:
THE YAKUMO:
1899 July 8 - Launched
1904 Feb. 9 - Russo-Japanese ar, Engagement
ofr Port Arthur
1904 Mar. 8 - Blookade of Port Arthur
" Aug. 10 - Engagement off Port Arthur
1905 May 27-28 - Participated in the Battle of
the Sea of Japan
1914 Aug. 23 - World War, Patrolled around Kiao-
Chow Bay.
n
Oot. 27 - Patrolled South Seas
1916 Sept. - Naval training ship
1917 Aug. -
1917 Oot. 20 - Patrolled Singopore Seotion
1923
Engaged in relief work at the time
of the great earthquake.
1918 to present - Served frequently es a training
ship for cadets.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
THE IWATE:
1900 Maroh 29 - Launohed
1904 Feb. 9 - Russo-Japanese War, Engagement off
Port Arthur
Aug. 14 - Engegement off Korean coast with
Russian Vladivostook fleet
1905 May 27-28- Battle in the Sea of Japan
1914 Aug. 23 - World War; pctrolled around Kiao-Chow
Bay
Dec. 17 - Disermed German converted cruiser off
Guem.
1916 Deo. 17 - On patrol in North Paoifio
1918 Sept. 5 - Petrolled Singapore Seotion
192l Mar. 1 - Nov. 1 - Joined the Third Fleet guarding
China coast.
1923 Sept. 1 - Engaged in relief work at the time of
the Great Enrthquake.
1915 to present - Served frequently as a training ship
for cadets.

Depertment of State
Beeeipt is asknowledged of the State Department note
of 15 May, file  melasBll/alt, which forwarded a copy of a
nete from the Japanese Ambassador at Washington relative to the
recent visits to Milo and Los Aageles by the Japanese tanker
onmo.
Binee the receipt of this note, information has been
reeeived from the Commandant of the Fourteenth Naval District
at Pearl Harbor, T. H., to the effect that he was notified on
14 April, l9ss, by the Japanese Consul General at Honelulu,
894.3311/
regarding the Celay in arrival at Hilo of the ONDo. Your
attention 1s invited to the fact, however, that the NDo was
originally seheduled to arrive at Hilo on 13 April, so that,
not withstanding the fact that oorrect information of her late
arrival had been carried in the local Japanese press on 24 March,
official information was not given the Commandant until the day
after her seheduled arrival. No official notification was given
the Commandant of the Eleventh Naval District at San Diego, Calif.,
regarding the delay in arrival of the ONDo at Los Angeles.
294
This department considers it most desirable that official
notification of changes in soheduled visits of foreign public
vessels be made, whenever ciroumstances permit, striotly in acoordance
with the request of the Secretary of State in his diplomatic memorandum
of 13 July, l9s5, and that the use of local notification for such
changes be restricted to a minimm.
Respectfully,
AND
CONFEEENCE
Dil
LEPT.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that a Japanese naval
training squadron consisting of the over-age cruisers
IVATE and YAKUMO under the command of Vice-Admiral Zengo
Yoshida sailed from Japan on June 9 on a foreign cruise
JL.7 1936
FILED
which will include visits at ports on both the Pacific
and Atlantic seaboards of the United States.
1
The itinerary of the cruise, as given to the Embassy's
Naval Attaché by the Japanese Navy Ministry, is as follows:
Arrive

Yokosuka
Although it is not thought by the Embassy that the
cruise of the Japanese naval training squadron has any
unusual political significance, both the JAPAN TIMES and
the NICHI NICHI of June 9 emphasized the point that this
is the first visit of a Japanese naval squadron to the
Atlantic coast of the United States since the Manchurian
Incident, the last cruise having been that of Admiral
Kichisaburo Nomura in 1929. Importance is attached to
the present cruise by these newspapers, which state that
Vice-Admiral Yoshida expects to call upon President Roosevelt,
the Secretary of State, the Secretary of the Navy, Admiral
Standley, and the Presidents of Mexico and Panama and will
utilize every opportunity to explain Japan's "true position"
in international affairs and to promote mutual understand-
ing. In a newspaper interview given immediately prior to
his departure Vice-Admiral Yoshida was quoted by the JAPAr
ADVERTISER of June 10 as follows: "In America I may have
opportunities to meet the President, the Secretary of State,
the

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
the Secretary of the Navy and many other prominent per-
sons. As I am not a diplomat I have nothing to achieve
in conneotion with diplomatic affairs, but as an officer
I shall try to make those people see the true situation
of Japan. I carry no message, though it seems to me
that many foreigners misunderstand Japan and that it is
necessary to wipe out such wrong ideas".
The Commander of the squadron, who is well-known to
the Embassy's Naval Attaché, is affable and friendly and
has a slight knowledge of English. He has never been in
the United States. In a recent conversation with the
Naval Attaché, Vice-Admiral Yoshida made no mention of
interviews with the President, the Secretary of the Navy,
and other officials or of any special political purpose
connected with his cruise to American waters.
Respectfully yours,
Not. Tran
Joseph C. Grew
833.

Othe afterndon of June 29, apparently
following reeeipt by the Mayor of Seattle of
the Department's telegram of June 29, Senator

and Major Sullivan urging that the Federal
Government grant permission for the offieers
and men of the Japanese training squadron to
participate in the Fourth of July parade at
Seattle. The text of the telegram received
by Senator Bone, as read over the telephone
to PC, is attached. Senator Bone's office
had also received an additional telegram in
regard to this matter from the Seattle Chamber
of Commeroe.
Subsequently, on the same afternoon,
Senator Sohwellenbach telephoned Mr. Hamilton
in regard to similar telegrams received by
Senator Sohwellenbach. The telegram from the
Seattle Chamber of Commerce contained one
important statement not inoluded in the other
telegram, namely, that the Japanese Admiral
had accepted the invitation extended to him by
the American Legion of Seattle. Mr. Hamilton
explained to Senator Sohwellenbach the general
practice in matters of this sort and told the
3enator that we had not, when the Department
sent its telegram of June 29 to the Mayor of
Seattle, known that the invitation had already
been

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)

been extended. Senator Sohwellenbach pointed
out the unfortunate reperoussions that might

consideration be given to the whole matter
with a view to avoiding possible international
embarrassment. Mr. Hamilton assured the
Senator that the concerned Departments, the
State, War and Navy Departments, would give
such further consideration.
On the night of June 29 the Department
received a further telegram from the Mayor of
Seattle.
On the morning of June 30 this matter was

Canaga of the Navy Department, Colonel Linooln

Department. Those present at this conference
approved a draft telegram to the Mayor of

Dear Mr. Secretary:

With reference to the conversation of this date
between the Chief, Military Intelligence Division, War Depart-
894.3311/320
ment, and the Chief of the Far Eastern Division, Department of
State, the Secretary of War requests me to inform you that the
War Department views with disfavor any parede of foreign troops
under arms on American soil, except under special circumstances.
The War Department's interpretation of "special circumstances"
is that no objection would be interposed in the case of Canadian
or Latin American troops.
Sincerely yours,

From:
Chief of Naval ObermAipesonS=
AJUL101936
To:
Commandants all Naval Districts.
Commander-in-Chief, U. S. Fleet.
Department of State
Commander Training Squadron.
SUBJECT:
Policy regarding participation in parades by naval forces.
Reference: (a) U. S. Navy Regulations, 1920.
1.
Recent inquiries have been received concerning the policy
894.3311/327
ef the Navy Department regarding participation in parades by naval forces.
For your information and guidance, yu are informed that it has been the
long-established policy of this department to restrict participation by
naval forces to parades in celebration of
(a) National holidays,
(b) Commemarative dates of local and nonpartisan interests,
(c) Occasions sponsored by national military or patrictic
societies,
(d) Civic celebrations noncommercial and nonpartisan in
character,
(e) Funeral ceremonies of national personages and military-
naval officials.
2.
The above statement of policy shall not be considered as
modifying any existing provisions of reference (a).
JL11936
FILED
(Signed) W. H. Standley.
CC: Comdt. Navy Yard, Wash.D.C.

Dear Mr. Bannerman:-
894.3311
Inviting attention to the attached letter from Mr.
Masutaro Inoue, Acting Consul General of Japan, in which my
atteation is called to the arrival at the Port of New York of two
Japanese vessels due the latter part of August. The itinerary
of the squadron is also supplied.
The Acting Consul General
requests the cooperation of this office in extending possible
894.3311
facilities and courtesies during the stay of the officers in this
eity.
A formal acknowledgment of the official's letter was
made in which it was stated that I would be pleased to comply with
his request, assuming, of course, that the Naval officers during
their brief stay here would be guests of the city and not the
govermment. For this reason coupled with the fact that the
5
contemplated arrival of the ships as late as August 25th, the matter
has not heretofore been communicated to you.
2
8
I may state further that it has been the practice of
this consulate as well as a number of others in this city when
poliee protection is desired for their visiting countrymen, instead
of submitting the request directly to the Police Depertment to ask
us, to do so and I have heretofore accommodated them, invariably
JUL·11936
notifying you at the time the request was made or subsequent to the
arrival or departure of the visitors. The Police Commissioner and
Chief Inspector underatend this situation fully and are glad to have
it thus.
In my phone talks with the Acting Conmul General
recently he seems to be under the impression now that the coming of
this squadron is an event of such momentous importance that in due
course the Ambassador will personally bring the matter to the attention
of the President.

July 25, 1936.
PC:
There is now pending with the Japanese
Government the question of the entry of
American public vessels into unopened
Japanese ports. In the circumstances, the
Department's reply to the Japanese Embassy
with rogard to the proposed visits of the
SHINTOKU MARU to ports in the Hawaiian
Islands should not express definite refusal
to permit the entry of that vessel into the
port of Nawiliwil1. It is suggested that
the reply should first dispose of the
proposed visit of the vessel to Hilo, and
that, with reference to the proposed visit
to Nawiliwili, it should contain some state-
ment substantially as follows:
"The proposed visit to
Nawiliwili, which is not a regular
port of entry and at which there are
no naval, customs or immigration
officials to extend the usual
facilities, is under consideration,
and a further communication will be
addressed to the Ambassador as soon
as decision has been reached in
regard to this matter."
894.3311/330

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 22, 1936.
8:
Referring to the suggestion contained in
Mr. Gray's memorandum of September 2l, it seems
to me that it would be preferable not to include
the suggested sentence. You will note from
Mr. Hornbeok's memorandum of July 28, especially
the tagged portion, that the question of delay
had already been discussed among officers of
the interested Departments and that while there
was some unfortunate delay in this Department
there was also delay in other Departments and
also delay on the part of the Commandant of
the Thirteenth Naval District in reporting the
matter to the Navy Department. It would seem
preferable to avoid in this letter any
contentious discussion or assumption of sole
blame, even by implication, by this Department,
especially in the light of the fact that this
phase of the matter has already been discussed
thoroughly in oral conversations.

Mr. Dooman.
Subject: Visits of Japanese training ship SHINTOKU
8
MARU to Hilo and Nawiliwil1.
Mr. Okazaki stated over the telephone that a report
3
3
had been received by the Embassy from the Master of the
Japanese training ship SHINTOKU MARU to the effect that
334
the vessel had arrived at Hilo and that, by reason of
its early arrival at that port, it would leave a few
days before the date scheduled. In all probability the
SHINTOKU MARU would sa1l from Hilo on August 12.
Mr. Okazaki referred to the Department's note in which it
was stated that the question of the visit of the vessel
to Nawiliwili was still under consideration, and he asked
whether it would be possible to receive before its departure
from Hilo a definitive reply to the request for permission
for the vessel to enter Nawiliwili.
Mr. Dooman stated that he hoped that it might be
possible for the Department to arrive at a decision today.
156
He said that in any oase he would notify Mr. Okazaki
F月
by telephone as soon as a decision had been reached, such
informal reply to be confirmed later by note.

Reference: Memorandum dated 17 July, 1936
From Commandant, Thirteenth
Naval Distriot, to Chief of
Naval Oporations.
Uepartment of State
Subjeot: Japanese Participation 1n Fourth
of July Parade, Seattle, Washington.
There are noted the statements in this memorandum
that, "rumor that foreign sailors had been invited to
894.33
parade in Seattle came to the Commandant the last week
in May"; that, "the Commandant was informed that, follow-
ing his suggestion, on l6 June the Mayor of Soattle ad-
dressed a letter to the State Department requesting the
necessary authority"; and that, "on 30 June," the
345
Commandant advised the Navy Department that he "was of
the opinion that to deny permission to parade might create
an exceedingly unpleasant situation".
Viewing these dates, it appears that, although, as
shown in other entries in the Commandant's momorandum
FILED
under reference, the Commandant was throughout of the
C
sailors in the parade should be sought from and should
be given by the Department of State, there passed more
than a month between the time when the Commandant first
had knowledge that the local organization had invited or
F/
was
F
G

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
was planning to invite foreign sailors to parade and the
time when the Commandant expressed to the Navy Depart-
ment his views with regard to the matter.
In paragraph 5 of the memorandum it is recorded that
on the day after the Commandant's advice to the Navy
Department that authorization should be given "a dispatch
was received from the State Department granting permission
for the Japanese sailors to parade". As a matter of fact,
the Department of State did not at any time "grant permis-
sion". In the telegram which the Department of State sent
to the Mayor of Seattle on July 30 the essential paragraphs
read as follows:
"Under the ciroumstanoes of the present case,
invitation having been extended, it would appear
that those responsible for its issuance have made
a commitment, that this cannot well be reoalled,
and that they should stand by the invitation.
This, however, will have to be on their own
responsibility. (Undersooring now added.)
"We suggest that you and Legion authorities
oonfer with Governor of the State of Hashington
and highest officers present of United States
Army and Navy and devise such arrangements as
may seem appropriate and practicable. There is,
of course, no objection to participation by
Japanese admiral in the review, and extending
of special courtesies and hospitality to his
staff, cadets and men seems highly desirable."
The Department also requested of the Navy Department and
the War Department that texts of this telegram be telegraphed
by those Departments to officers of the two Departments
respeotively who would be concerned at Seattle.
In

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
In paragraph 7 of the memorandum the Commandant
makes the comment, "It is evident that to comply with
international usage prompt action on the part of the
State Department is desirable in replying to interroga-
tions such as those addressed to it when the Japanese
ships approached Seattle." The first information which
the Department of State had with regard to this matter
came with receipt by the Department of State on June 22
of a letter from the Mayor of Seattle, dated June 16,
which the Commandant mentions. On June 24, the Department
of State communicated by letter with the Navy Department
and the iar Department. On the same day, the Department
of State wrote to the Mayor of Seattle acknowledging the
Mayor's letter of June 16 and stating that the Mayor's re-
quest was being given considerution by the appropriate
authorities and as soon as a decision should be reached
would communicate further with the Mayor. Thereafter, the
Department of State received from the War Department under
date June 26 a letter in which it was stated, ". . . . the
War Department believes it inadvisable for any foreign body
of troops to be allowed to participate in any parade on
Amerioan soil while under arms"; and, the Department of
State received from the Navy Department under date June 29
a letter in which it was stated that, "The Navy Department.
interposes no objeotion to this partioipation by Japanese
troops

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
troops in the Fourth of July parade. However, it is
considered inadvisable at this time to create suoh a
precedent . . . ." In the interval, officers of the
Department of State had brought this matter informally
to the attention of offioers of the Navy Department and
of the War Department; and the Department of State had
received several telegrams from the Mayor and Committee
members in Seattle and several communications from offi-
cials of the State of Washington present in Washington,
D. C. On June 29, after full consideration with officors
of the Navy Department and of the War Department, the
Department of State telegraphed to the Mayor of Seattle
that the matter had been given very careful consideration
and, "many factors having been weighed, the conclusion
has been reaohed that the participation under reference
would not be advisable. Authorization therefore cannot
be given . . . ."
Thereafter, immediately upon receipt of information
that Seattle Post No. 1, American Legion, had already
extended invitation on behalf of the City of Seattle and
that the making of arrangements had gone far, officers
of the Department of State again consulted informally with
officers of the Navy Department and the War Department,
and, on June 30, the Department of State telegraphed the
Mayor

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
一 5 -
Mayor of Seattle, as per quotation given above, advising
that those responsible for issuing the invitation stand
by the invitation but that their action would "have to
be on their own responsibility" and suggesting that they
confer with officials and ofrioers locally present and
devise suoh arrangements as might seem appropriate and
praoticable.
In the light of the above, it is evident that there
was delay. It appears that, in Seattle, neither the local
Committee nor the Mayor acted promptly on the Commandant's
suggestion that the State Department should be consulted;
and the Commandant did not report promptly to the Navy
Department. In Washington, the Department of State, in-
stead of taking the matter up immediately by oral communica-
tion with the Navy Department and the War Department, relied
upon the ordinary procedure of written communioations; and
the Navy Department and the War Department responded by the
same procedure. That the delay was unfortunate and reliance
on this procedure not satisfaotory was given reoognition in
Washington when, in a conference between officers of the
Department of State and of the Navy and of the Army on
June 30, an officer of the Department of State remarked the
delay and the unsatisfactory character of this procedure
and made the suggestion that, when such cases arise, the
Department to the attention of which the case first comes
might to advantage at once take the matter up orally with
the other Departments concerned, which view was concurred
1n

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
in by all of the officers of the three Departments then
prosont.
Further, in the light of the above, it is believed
that the Commandant's apparent view, as implied in para-
graph 7 of the memorandum under reference, that the
Department of State was negligent and was alone responsible
for the delays whioh ooourred in this oase is not warranted.
It is now suggested that (1) in the interest of all
concerned, when officers in the field funotioning under
any one of the three Departments receive knowledge locally
of oases, situations or developments such as or similar to
those involved in this case, such officers should report
promptly thereon, stating the facts and submitting their
suggestions, to the Departments, respeotively, under which
they funotion; and that (2) when any suoh oases, situations
or developments are brought to the knowledge of any one
of the three Dopartments, that Department immediately take
the matter up orally with the other Departments conoerned.

Department of State
Reference: Memorandum of July 28 in comment upon
memorandum of July 17 from the Commandant
of the Thirteenth Naval Distriot, on the
subjeot: Japanese participation in Fourth
of July parade, Seattle, Jashington.
l. In regard to the question of "oompliance with
894.33
intornational usage", it may be pointed out that, with
due and full regard for requirements of international
C
usage, a question of a possible parading by foreign troops
on Amerioan soil would need to be made subjeot of con-
3
sultation and agreement between the Amerioan Government
46
and the government of the foreign country the parading
of whose troops is under consideration. No invitation
should be extended by local authorities either to the
troops or to the foreign government conoerned. An invita-
tion, if issued, should originate with or be p&ssed through
the Amerioan Government. the agenoy to be used baing the
Department of State. If the initiative is taken by aliens?
3
the suggestion made by such aliens should be direoted to
C
1936
their own government, whioh government should address any
suggestions or inquiries whioh it may feel moved to make
to the American Government, the appropriate addressee being
the Seoretary of State or an Amerioan ambassador or minister
abroad. It should not be expeoted that the American Govern-
G
ment

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
mont will, through the agenoy of the Department of State,
direotly address to foreign troops or authorize American
citizens to address to foreign troops invitations to parade
on American soil. Suoh invitations, if and when issued,
should be addressed by the American Government to the
foreign government oonoerned.
2. On the express question of general polioy with
regard to parading of foreign troops under arms on Ameri-
can soil, there has been consultation among officers of
the Department of State, of the War Department and of the
Navy Department, and there has been some exohange of
written communications. The consensus of opinion thus
arrived at is that it is as a general rule not desirable
that foreign troops be asked to or be permitted to parade
on Amorican soil, but that, as express exceptions to this
rule, there should be no objection under ordinary ciroum-
stances to issuance, through proper channels, of such
invitations to Canadian troops, as in the past, and to
possible issuance of such invitations to troops of other
countries of North and South Amorioa.

Embassy of The
United States of America
Tokyo, October 10, 1936.
No. 619.
Exoellency:
I have the honor to inform Your Exoellency that the
Chamber of Commeroe of Port Arthur, Texas, has requested
that an invitation be extended for the visit of a vessel
of the Japanese Navy to that port. While it was originally
intended that this invitation should be extended to the
Japanese cruisers YAkUMO and IWATE, which are at present
in American waters, the Japanese Embassy at Washington has
advised my Government informally that the present schedule
of these ships would not permit of a visit at this time.
The Port Arthur Chamber of Commerce now wishes an in-
vitation extended to the Japanese Government for a visit
at some future date when a Japanese vessel may be in nearby
waters, and the invitation has been indorsed by the Governor
of Teras.
Acting under instruotions of my Government I have the
honor accordingly to extend this invitation to Your Exoellency
for transmission to the appropriate authorities of the Japanese
Government. In so doing, however, it is desired that I make
it clear that this is not an official invitation on the part
of my Government, but that it comes from the Chamber of Commerce
of
His Excellency
Mr. Hachiro Arita,
His Imperial Japanese Majesty's
Minister for Foreign Affairs,
otc.,
eto.
eto.,

of Port Arthur.

No. 105, American I.
Ministry of Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo, November 10, 1936.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to your note No. 619 of
October 10 informing me that the Chamber of Commeroe of
Port Arthur, State of Texas, has requested that an invita-
tion be extended to the Japanese authorities for the visit
of a vessel of the Japanese Navy to that port at some future
date when a Japanese naval vessel may be in nearby waters. In
reply I have the honor to state that the Japanese Government
highly appreciates the proposal of the Port Arthur Chamber
of Commerce but that at the present time no plan to have
Japanese warships cruising in the waters off the easten
coast of the United States is scheduled. This invitation
will, however, be taken up for consideration when any such
plan is under contemplation in the future. I hope you will
kindly communicate this to the Port Arthur Chamber of Commerce.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew, Sir, the
assurances of my highest consideration.
Hachiro Arita (SEAL)
Minister for Foreign Affairs
Erle R. Dickover, Esquire,
Chargé d'Arfaires ad interim
of the United States of America,
Tokyo.

Because of the fact that special maneuvers are being
conducted in the San Pedro-San Clemente Island-San Diego aree,
during the period 1 February-5 March, 1937, it is considered
undesirable that visits of foreign public vessels be permitted
in that area between the dates mentioned.

ment be informed that the proposed visits to Los Angeles will
not be convenient.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 18, 1937.
On January 2l Mr. Cohn called on
Mr. Moore. During the latter part of the
call Mr. Hamilton was present. Mr. Cohn
said that he was on friendly terms with the
Japanese Consul at Los Angeles and it was
suggested to Mr. Cohn that he take up with
the Japanese Consul the matter of the
smuggling of sake from Japanese naval tankers
in harbor at San Pedro. It was suggested
also to Mr. Cohn that if such an approach
by him to the Consul should not result in
the cessation of this smuggling, Mr. Cohn
bring the matter to the attention of the
Department which would take the matter up
with the Japanese Embassy here.

and crew of the Japanese tanker ERIMO. The Navy Depart-

11 May, 1937, on the subject of proposed visits of the Japanese

894.3311/401

that the Navy Department may possibly be willing to exchange
monthly figures of the purchases of fuel for the Japanese Navy
in the United States against like figures of purchases of fua
for the Japanese navy in the Netherlands Indies.
I will be glad to bring this suggestion to the
attention of the Netherlands authorities and will let you know as
aoon as I have heard from then.
Sincerely yours,
Mr.Pierrepont Moffat
